Factor Augmented Matrix Regression
Elynn Y. Chen, Jianqing Fan, Xiaonan Zhu

Abstract
We introduce F actor- A ugmented Ma trix R egression (FAMAR) to address the growing applications of matrix-variate data and their associated challenges, particularly with high-dimensionality and covariate correlations. FAMAR encompasses two key algorithms. The first is a novel non-iterative approach that efficiently estimates the factors and loadings of the matrix factor model, utilizing techniques of pre-training, diverse projection, and block-wise averaging. The second algorithm offers an accelerated solution for penalized matrix factor regression. Both algorithms are supported by established statistical and numerical convergence properties. Empirical evaluations, conducted on synthetic and real economics datasets, demonstrate FAMAR’s superiority in terms of accuracy, interpretability, and computational speed. Our application to economic data showcases how matrix factors can be incorporated to predict the GDPs of the countries of interest, and the influence of these factors on the GDPs.



Keywords:
Matrix factor models; Matrix regression; Factor-augmented regression; Diversified projections; High-dimensionality.

1
Introduction

Matrix-variate data are commonly encountered in diverse domains nowadays, such as finance, economics, spatio-temporal data, healthcare, images, and social networks
(
Chen et al., 2020
;
Chen et al., 2023
)
. In many problems, especially in high-dimensional regimes, it is common that matrix covariates assume low-rank structure or can be well approximated by low-rank matrices as exemplified by factor models
(
Wainwright, 2019
;
Chen & Fan, 2021
)
. The outcomes of interest are directly influenced by latent low-rank matrix factors, not by the observable matrix covariates themselves. For instance, autism diagnosis relies on identifying specific patterns within brain connectivity networks, underscoring a direct link to reduced-dimensionality factors
(
Nogay & Adeli, 2020
)
; regional GDP is shaped by the condensed economic indicators found within spatial-temporal housing data
(
Kopoin et al., 2013
)
; and the GDP growth of countries is determined by underlying economic factors, demonstrating a direct relationship with these distilled elements
(
Banerjee et al., 2005
)
.

To incorporate the matrix factor structure in matrix regressions,
we introduce the
F
actor
A
ugmented
Ma
trix
R
egression (FAMAR) for observed random variables
\left\{\left(y_{i},\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right),\;i\in[n]\right\}
, in which
y_{i}
is a scalar response of interest and
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
is a
p_{1}\times p_{2}
covariate matrix, defined as

\begin{cases}&y_{i}=\left\langle\boldsymbol{A}^{*},\boldsymbol{F}_{i}\right\rangle+\left\langle\boldsymbol{B}^{*},\boldsymbol{U}_{i}\right\rangle+\varepsilon_{i},\qquad\text{\it Matrix Factor Regression (MFR)}\\
&\boldsymbol{X}_{i}=\boldsymbol{R}^{*}\boldsymbol{F}_{i}\boldsymbol{C}^{*\top}+\boldsymbol{U}_{i},\qquad\qquad\hskip 14.63881pt\text{\it Matrix Factor Model (MFM)}\end{cases}

(1)

where
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
is the
{k_{1}\times k_{2}}
latent factor matrix of lower dimensions
k_{j}\ll p_{j}
for
j=1,2
,
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
is the unobserved idiosyncratic matrix, and
\boldsymbol{R}^{*}
of dimension
p_{1}\times k_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{C}^{*}
of dimension
p_{2}\times k_{2}
are the loading matrices of the Matrix Factor Model (MFM)
(
Chen & Fan, 2021
)
. The impact of covariate matrix
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
on outcome
y_{i}
is through the latent factors
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
and unobserved idiosyncratic matrix
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
with
matrices
\boldsymbol{A}^{*}
of dimension
k_{1}\times k_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
of dimension
p_{1}\times p_{2}
as the regression
coefficient matrices of the Matrix Factor Regression (MFR). As the first component in MFR is the low-dimensional factor effect, the coefficient
\boldsymbol{A}^{*}
can be estimated without any structures. We consider both sparse and low-rank structure for the regression coefficient matrix
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
since it is high-dimensional.

As practitioners harness the power of various data, model (
1
) has wide applications in economics and finance. For example,
y_{i}
represents the monthly GDP of a country of interest, and
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times p_{2}}
consists of the
p_{2}
economic variables collected from
p_{1}
different countries.
Model (
1
) is able to separate economic common factor
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
and idiosyncratic shocks
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
and provide economists with their respective impacts on the GDP of one country of interest.
A salient feature of model (
1
) is that the association between the response
y_{i}
and the covariate matrix
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
is established directly through the factor
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
and idiosyncratic matrix
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
.
This structure makes covariate inputs much weakly correlated and relates to various traditional models, as illustrated below.

Example 1

(Matrix regression with MFM covariates)
.

Matrix regression is written as

\begin{cases}&y_{i}=\left\langle\boldsymbol{B}^{*},\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right\rangle+\varepsilon_{i},\hskip 25.83325pt\text{\it Matrix Regression (MR)}\\
&\boldsymbol{X}_{i}=\boldsymbol{R}^{*}\boldsymbol{F}_{i}\boldsymbol{C}^{*\top}+\boldsymbol{U}_{i},\hskip 12.91663pt\text{\it Matrix Factor Model (MFM)}\end{cases}

(2)

Model (
2
) is a special case of model (
1
) with
\boldsymbol{A}^{*}={\boldsymbol{R}^{*}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\boldsymbol{C}^{*}
.

While we directly observe
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
, using the matrix regression model (
2
) might seem straightforward. The influence of the latent factor
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
in model (
2
) is represented as
{\boldsymbol{R}^{*}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\boldsymbol{C}^{*}
. However, the general FAMAR model introduces
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{*}:=\boldsymbol{A}^{*}-\boldsymbol{R}^{\top}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\boldsymbol{C}
, which quantifies the additional impact of the latent factor
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
in model (
1
) that is not accounted for by the observed predictor
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
in model (
2
).

Example 2

(Reduce-rank matrix regression)
.

Low-rank matrix regression
y_{i}=\left\langle\boldsymbol{B}^{*},\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right\rangle+\varepsilon_{i}
corresponds to a special case of (
1
) with low-rank coefficient matrix
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
, matrix factor
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}=0
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}=\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
when
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
does not admit a factor structure, and
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{*}=0
when it does.

Example 3

(Sparse matrix regression)
.

Sparse matrix regression
y_{i}=\left\langle\boldsymbol{B}^{*},\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right\rangle+\varepsilon_{i}
is a special case of model (
1
) with sparse coefficient matrix
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
, and
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
has not low-rank component, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}=0
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}=\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
.

There are several advantages of considering the more general model (
1
). First, in reality, especially when the columns and rows of
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
are highly correlated, the leading factors are likely to make additional contributions to the outcome, beyond a fixed portion represented by
\boldsymbol{A}^{*}={\boldsymbol{R}^{*}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\boldsymbol{C}^{*}
in model (
2
).
To this end, model (
1
) involves incorporating the primary factors into a low-rank regression that extends the linear space defined by
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
in useful ways by noting that using
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
as covariates are the same as the usage of
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
. In addition, even when model (
2
) is correctly specified, model (
1
) is helpful in dealing with the colinearity of
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
due to the factor structure
(
Fan, Ke & Wang, 2020
)
.
This is also confirmed in our empirical analysis. When covariates
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
are highly correlated, direct low-rank regression on
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
’s often overestimates the rank due to their dependency structure, reducing the effectiveness of regularization and the interpretability of estimation.
FAMAR enhances performance by utilizing the space spanned by
(\boldsymbol{F}_{i},\boldsymbol{U}_{i})
, which is the same as that span by
(\boldsymbol{F}_{i},\boldsymbol{X}_{i})
, with augmented feature
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
, capitalizing further on underlying information. Additionally, its factor model approach decorrelates the covariance matrix, leading to coefficient estimators that are both more interpretable and stable.

Factor augmentations in the vector contexts have been studied under various settings and shown to be powerful
(
Fan, Ke & Wang, 2020
;
Zhou et al., 2021
;
Fan & Gu, 2022
;
Fan et al., 2023
)
.
In contrast, little work has been done on the matrix-variate problems.
Matrix-variate settings are intrinsically different because of their multidimensional structure, where low rankness comes into play.
Linear and generalized matrix regression have been studied in
Zhou & Li (2014)
;
Wang et al. (2017)
;
Fan et al. (2019)
.
However, model (
1
) in the present paper is different in that the association between
y_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
is not direct as those in the literature but rather through the latent variables
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
.
We present in the real data analysis that such an association provides better prediction and interpretation of the effect of common economic factors and idiosyncratic variables.
In addition, existing estimation methods solving model (
1
) MFM entail spectral or iterative optimization methods
(
Chen et al., 2019
;
Chen & Fan, 2021
;
Liu & Chen, 2022
;
Yu et al., 2022
)
.
We propose a new non-iterative method, consisting of simple procedures of “Pre-Trained Projection” and “Block-Average-an-OLS”, to obtain good estimators of
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
from the MFM, which are of independent interest.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows.
Section
2
develops the FAMAR method in details, including a new method for estimating the MFM parameters in model (
1
) in Section
2.1
and an efficient algorithm for solving MFR parameters in model (
1
) in Section
2.2
.
Section
3
establishes their theoretical properties.
Sections
4
and
5
present empirical results on synthetic and real datasets.
Section
6
concludes and discusses future works.
All proofs are relegated to the supplementary material.

2
Factor-Augmented Matrix Regression

When the factors
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
and the residuals
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
are directly observable, unknown coefficients in model (
1
) can be obtained by a general matrix regression
(
Wainwright, 2019
)
.
In the more challenging setting where
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
are not directly observed, we need first to estimate
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
from the observed matrix variate
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
.
Section
2.1
introduces a novel non-iterative estimation approach to obtain estimated
\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{i}
and
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
in model (
1
) that achieve optimal convergence rates.
With these, to facilitate the estimation of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
under the high-dimensional setting, we could impose either low-rank or sparse structure on
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
.

FAMAR with Low-Rank
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
.

The low-rank structure on
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
can be achieved by solving
nuclear norm regularized matrix regression
with an accelerated algorithm introduced in Section
2.2
:

\big(\widehat{\boldsymbol{A}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}\big)\in\argmin_{\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}}\left\{\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(y_{i}-\left\langle\boldsymbol{A},\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{i}\right\rangle-\left\langle\boldsymbol{B},\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{i}\right\rangle\right)^{2}+\lambda_{n}\|\boldsymbol{B}\|_{*}\right\},

(3)

where
\|\boldsymbol{B}\|_{*}
denotes the nuclear norm of the matrix coefficient.

FAMAR with Sparse
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
.

The overall sparsity structure on
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
can be induced by using the
\ell_{1}
norm on vectorized
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
, akin to factor augmented regression in vector form
(
Fan, Ke & Wang, 2020
;
Fan et al., 2023
)
. Specifically, the overall sparsity on
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
can be achieved by solving the following optimization problem

\big(\widehat{\boldsymbol{A}}^{*},\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}^{*}\big)\in\argmin_{\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}}\left\{\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(y_{i}-\left\langle\boldsymbol{A},\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{i}\right\rangle-\left\langle\boldsymbol{B},\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{i}\right\rangle\right)^{2}+\lambda_{n}\|\vect(\boldsymbol{B})\|_{1}\right\}.

(4)

We also note that FAMAR provides a broader range of sparsity options compared to its vector-based counterpart, thanks to the multi-dimensional nature of the coefficient matrix
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
. This allows for the imposition of row-wise or column-wise sparsity in
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
, an option not available in vector-based models.
We establish theoretical guarantees for the overall sparsity structure with LASSO in Section
3.3.2
. Theoretical analysis on FAMAR with row-wise or column-wise sparsity in
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
can be carried out by applying, e.g.,
Yuan & Lin (2006)
;
Obozinski et al. (2011)
;
Hastie et al. (2015)
in the theoretical frameworks presented therein and are left for future research.

2.1
Pre-Trained Projection and Block-Wise Averaging

We present an innovative non-iterative estimation approach that utilizes pre-training and exploits the unique structure inherent in Kronecker products.
It consists of two major steps, namely “Pre-Trained Projection”, where we estimate the latent factor matrix
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
by a one-time projection, and “Block-Averaging-an-OLS”, where we exploit the unique Kronecker structure and estimate by simple averaging in a block-wise fashion.

Estimating matrix factors by “Pre-Trained Projection”.

The idea of
pre-trained projection
is inspired by the diversified projection proposed by
Fan & Liao (2022)
for the vector factor models.
Here for the matrix factor model, we introduce
“row and column diversified projection matrices”

\boldsymbol{W}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{W}_{2}
in Definition
4
.
Accordingly, we can obtain a crude estimator of the matrix factor

\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{i}=\frac{1}{p_{1}p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\boldsymbol{W}_{2}.

(5)

Following from the matrix factor structure of
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
, we have

\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{i}=\boldsymbol{H}_{1}\boldsymbol{F}_{i}\boldsymbol{H}_{2}^{\top}+\frac{1}{p_{1}p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{W}_{1}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}_{i}\boldsymbol{W}_{2},

(6)

where
\boldsymbol{H}_{1}=(p_{1})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{H}_{2}=(p_{2})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{2}^{\top}\boldsymbol{C}^{*}
are transformation matrices. A rotated version of
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
(i.e.
\boldsymbol{H}_{1}\boldsymbol{F}_{i}\boldsymbol{H}_{2}^{\top}
) can be estimated by
\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{i}
as long as the second idiosyncratic term in (
6
) is averaged away and the first signal term dominates the average of the noise in the second term. Formally, we characterize the desirable projection matrices as follows.

Definition 4

(Diversified projection matrices)
.

Let
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
be universal positive constants. Matrices
\boldsymbol{W}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times k_{1}}
and
\boldsymbol{W}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{2}\times k_{2}}
are said to be diversified projection matrices for MFM in (
1
) if they satisfy

(a)

\|\boldsymbol{W}_{1}\|_{\max}<c_{1}
and
\|\boldsymbol{W}_{2}\|_{\max}<c_{2}
, where
\|\boldsymbol{W}\|_{\max}
is the element-wise
\ell_{\infty}
-norm.

(b)

The matrices
\boldsymbol{H}_{1}=(p_{1})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}\times k_{1}}
and
\boldsymbol{H}_{2}=(p_{2})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{2}^{\top}\boldsymbol{C}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}\times k_{2}}
satisfy
\nu_{\min}\big(\boldsymbol{H}_{1}\big)\gg p_{1}^{-1/2}
and
\nu_{\min}\big(\boldsymbol{H}_{2}\big)\gg p_{2}^{-1/2}
, respectively.

(c)

\boldsymbol{W}_{1},\boldsymbol{W}_{2}
are independent of
\{\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
.

Diversified projection matrices can be achieved in various ways
(
Fan & Liao, 2022
;
Fan & Gu, 2022
)
, such as those based on observed characteristics, initial transformation, and the Hadamard projection.
We consider the idea of
“pre-trainning”
, which is a data-driven method of constructing
\boldsymbol{W}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{W}_{2}
by
\alpha
-PCA
(
Chen & Fan, 2021
)
through data splitting.
Specifically, with a separate independent set of samples
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{j}^{\prime},\ j\in[n^{\prime}]
, we obtain
\boldsymbol{W}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{W}_{2}
as the estimations of
\boldsymbol{R}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{C}^{*}
utilizing the method of
\alpha
-PCA. As condition
4
(b) does not require a consistent estimation, the pre-trained sample size
n^{\prime}
can be a negligible fraction of sample size
n
, as to be formally demonstrated.
Then, with the sample of interest
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i},\ i\in[n]
, we obtain an estimator of the matrix factor
\{\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{i}\}
,
i\in[n]
using equation (
5
) with
\boldsymbol{W}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{W}_{2}
as the diversified projection matrices.

Estimating the loading matrix by “Block-Averaging-an-OLS”.

To estimate
\boldsymbol{R}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{C}^{*}
, our proposed method averages over blocks of an OLS estimator of the vectorized MFM (
1
).
The details and rationales are explained as follows.
The vectorized model of MFM (
1
) with all samples stacked in rows can be written as
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{F}{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}}^{\top}+\mathbb{U}
, or equivalently,

\mathbb{X}=\big[\mathbb{F}(\boldsymbol{H}_{2}\otimes\boldsymbol{H}_{1})^{\top}\big]\big[\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}(\boldsymbol{H}_{2}^{-1}\otimes\boldsymbol{H}_{1}^{-1})\big]^{\top}+\mathbb{U}:=\mathring{\mathbb{F}}\;\mathring{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}^{\top}+\mathbb{U},

(7)

where
\boldsymbol{H}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{H}_{2}
are the transformation matrices defined in Definition
4
,

\mathbb{F}
is an
n\times k_{1}k_{2}
matrix whose
i
-th row is the vectorized
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
,
\mathbb{X}
and
\mathbb{U}
are
n\times p_{1}p_{2}
matrices defined similarly,
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}
is the true
p_{1}p_{2}\times k_{1}k_{2}
loading matrix satisfying
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}=\boldsymbol{C}^{*}\otimes\boldsymbol{R}^{*}
,

\mathring{\mathbb{F}}:=\mathbb{F}(\boldsymbol{H}_{2}\otimes\boldsymbol{H}_{1})^{\top}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathring{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}:=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}(\boldsymbol{H}_{2}^{-1}\otimes\boldsymbol{H}_{1}^{-1})

(8)

are the rotated truth that our pre-trained projected estimators are targeting at.

Now with the vectorized estimator

\widehat{\mathbb{F}}:=\big(\operatorname{vec}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{1}),\ldots,\operatorname{vec}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{n})\big)^{\top}
from the
“pre-trained projection”
that well approximates
\mathring{\mathbb{F}}
, the rotated loading matrix
\mathring{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}
can be well-approximated by an OLS:

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}\frac{1}{2n}\big\|\mathbb{X}-\widehat{\mathbb{F}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\big\|_{F}^{2}=\mathbb{X}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}(\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbb{F}})^{-1}.

(9)

Our estimation target
\mathring{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}=\mathring{\boldsymbol{C}}\otimes\mathring{\boldsymbol{R}}
naturally admits a Kronecker product structure with

\mathring{\boldsymbol{R}}=\boldsymbol{R}^{*}\boldsymbol{H}_{1}^{-1},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathring{\boldsymbol{C}}=\boldsymbol{C}^{*}\boldsymbol{H}_{2}^{-1},

(10)

which are the rotated truth. To take advantage of such a structure, we use block averaging to improve the quality of estimation.
Specifically,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}
can be divided into
p_{2}k_{2}
blocks of
p_{1}\times k_{1}
matrices and the
(i,j)
-th block is matrix
\mathring{c}_{ij}\mathring{\boldsymbol{R}}
, where
\mathring{c}_{ij}
is the
(i,j)
-th element of
\mathring{\boldsymbol{C}}
.
Denote
\boldsymbol{E}_{pq}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times pq}
as the matrix consisting of
q
blocks of
\boldsymbol{I}_{p}
, we have that

\frac{1}{p_{2}k_{2}}\boldsymbol{E}_{p_{1}p_{2}}\mathring{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}_{k_{1}k_{2}}^{\top}=\left(\frac{1}{p_{2}k_{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{p_{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{2}}\mathring{c}_{ij}\right)\mathring{\boldsymbol{R}}=:\mathring{c}_{s}\mathring{}\boldsymbol{R}

and

\frac{1}{p_{1}k_{1}}\boldsymbol{E}_{p_{2}p_{1}}\boldsymbol{S}_{p_{2}p_{1}}\mathring{}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\boldsymbol{S}_{k_{2}k_{1}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}_{k_{2}k_{1}}^{\top}=\left(\frac{1}{p_{1}k_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{p_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{1}}\mathring{r}_{ij}\right)\mathring{\boldsymbol{C}}=:\mathring{r}_{s}\mathring{}\boldsymbol{C},

where
\boldsymbol{S}_{pq}
is the shuffle matrix defined by taking slices of the
\boldsymbol{I}_{pq}
:

\mathbf{S}_{pq}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{I}_{pq}(1:q:pq,:)\\
\mathbf{I}_{pq}(2:q:pq,:)\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{I}_{pq}(q:q:pq,:)\end{array}\right].

MATLAB colon notation is used here to indicate submatrices, where
j:i:k
creates a regularly-spaced vector using
i
as the increment between elements.
This Kronecker structure enables us to refine
\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
by block-wise averaging.
We define the averaging estimator

\widehat{}\boldsymbol{R}:=\frac{1}{p_{2}k_{2}}\boldsymbol{E}_{p_{1}p_{2}}\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}_{k_{1}k_{2}}^{\top},\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{}\boldsymbol{C}=\frac{1}{p_{1}k_{1}}\boldsymbol{E}_{p_{2}p_{1}}\boldsymbol{S}_{p_{2}p_{1}}\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\top}\boldsymbol{S}_{k_{2}k_{1}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}_{k_{2}k_{1}}^{\top}.

(11)

Therefore, two constants
\mathring{c}_{s}
and
\mathring{r}_{s}
can be estimated by
\widehat{c}_{s}:=\frac{1}{p_{2}k_{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{p_{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{2}}\widehat{c}_{ij}
and
\widehat{r}_{s}:=\frac{1}{p_{1}k_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{p_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{1}}\widehat{r}_{ij}
, respectively, where
\widehat{c}_{ij}
(resp.
\widehat{r}_{ij}
) is the
(i,j)
-th element of
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{C}
(resp.
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{R}
).
Accordingly, the estimator of the idiosyncratic matrix
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
is given by

\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{i}:=\boldsymbol{X}_{i}-\frac{1}{\widehat{c}_{s}\widehat{r}_{s}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{i}\widehat{\boldsymbol{C}}^{\top},

(12)

where
\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{i}
is obtained by “pre-trained projection” and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{C}}
are estimated by “block-averaging-an-OLS”. Note that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{i}
,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}
, and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{C}}
estimate the affine transformed version of
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
,
\boldsymbol{R}^{*}
, and
\boldsymbol{C}^{*}
, while
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
directly estimates
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
.

2.2
Accelerated Algorithm for Solving Matrix Factor Regression

Using
\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{i}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{i}
estimated through
pre-trained projection
and
block-averaging-an-OLS
, we estimate the model coefficients
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{A}^{*}
for the MFR (
1
) by applying Nesterov’s accelerated iterative algorithm
(
Nesterov, 2013
;
Ji & Ye, 2009
)
to solve the optimization program (
3
). We explore both low-rank and sparse settings for
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
in our subsequent analysis.

Accelerated algorithm for low-rank coefficient matrix
\boldsymbol{B}
.

The process is detailed in Algorithm
1
as follows. We define the objective function from (
3
) as:

F_{n}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})=f_{n}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})+\lambda_{n}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{B}\right\rVert_{*},

where

f_{n}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}):=(2n)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(y_{i}-\left\langle\boldsymbol{A},\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{i}\right\rangle-\left\langle\boldsymbol{B},\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{i}\right\rangle\right)^{2}.

We now drop the
\widehat{\cdot}
in
\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{i}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{i}
and use
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{i}
and
{\boldsymbol{U}}_{i}
instead for clear presentation.
The accelerated algorithm keeps two sequences
\{(\boldsymbol{B}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{A}^{(k)})\}
and
\{(\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)})\}
and updated them iteratively.
At the beginning of the
k
-th step,
\{(\boldsymbol{A}^{(k-1)},\boldsymbol{B}^{(k-1)})\}
is the approximate solution from the last step, and
\{(\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)})\}
is the search point for the current step.
The accelerated algorithm performs the gradient descent update at the search point
\{(\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)})\}
.
Specifically, we define the update equations as

p_{L^{(k)}}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}):={\cal T}_{\lambda_{n}/L^{(k)}}\left(\boldsymbol{B}-\frac{1}{L^{(k)}}\nabla_{B}f_{n}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})\right),\quad q_{L^{(k)}}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}):=\boldsymbol{A}-\frac{1}{L^{(k)}}\nabla_{A}f_{n}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}),

where
L^{(k)}
is an appropriate step size,
{\cal T}_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{C})
is an operator defined as
{\cal T}_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{C})=\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{D}_{\lambda}\boldsymbol{V}^{\top}
on a matrix
\boldsymbol{C}
with SVD
\boldsymbol{C}=\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{D}\boldsymbol{V}^{\top}
and
\boldsymbol{D}_{\lambda}
is diagonal with the soft thresholding
(d_{\lambda})_{ii}=\sign(d_{ii})\max\{0,\left\lvert d_{ii}\right\rvert-\lambda\}
.
The solution for the
k
-the step is updated as

\boldsymbol{B}^{(k)}\leftarrow p_{L^{(k)}}(\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)}),\quad\boldsymbol{A}^{(k)}\leftarrow q_{L^{(k)}}(\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)}).

At last, the search point for the next step,
(\boldsymbol{P}^{(k+1)},\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k+1)})
, is constructed as a linear combination of the latest two approximate solutions at step
(k)
and
(k-1)
. Specifically,

(\boldsymbol{P}^{(k+1)},\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k+1)})\leftarrow(\boldsymbol{B}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{A}^{(k)})+\frac{\alpha^{(k)}-1}{\alpha^{(k+1)}}\big((\boldsymbol{B}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{A}^{(k)})-(\boldsymbol{B}^{(k-1)},\boldsymbol{A}^{(k-1)})\big),

(13)

where we set
\alpha^{(1)}=1
and update it iteratively by
\alpha^{(k+1)}=0.5\times(1+\sqrt{1+4(\alpha^{(k)})^{2}})
.
To choose an appropriate step size
L^{(k)}
, we start from an initial estimate
L_{0}
and increase this estimate with a multiplicative factor
\gamma>1
repeatedly until the convergence condition

\displaystyle F_{n}\big(q_{L}(\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)}),p_{L}(\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)})\big)

\displaystyle\geq f_{n}(\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)})+H_{L,B}(p_{L}(\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)});\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)})

(14)

\displaystyle+H_{L,A}(q_{L}(\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)});\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)})+\lambda_{n}\|p_{L}(\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)})\|_{*},

is satisfied for
L=L^{(k)}
. The
H_{L,A}(\boldsymbol{A}^{\prime};\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})
and
H_{L,B}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime};\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})
are defined, respectively, as

\displaystyle H_{L,A}(\boldsymbol{A}^{\prime};\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})

\displaystyle:=\left\langle\boldsymbol{A}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{A},\nabla_{A}f_{n}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})\right\rangle+\frac{L}{2}\|\boldsymbol{A}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{A}\|_{F}^{2}

(15)

\displaystyle H_{L,B}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime};\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})

\displaystyle:=\left\langle\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{B},\nabla_{B}f_{n}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})\right\rangle+\frac{L}{2}\|\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{B}\|_{F}^{2}.

Lemma
18
in the supplementary shows that Algorithm
1
converges under condition (
14
).

An alternative numerical method involves initially estimating
\boldsymbol{A}^{*}
using linear regression on the vectorized form of MFM (
1
), followed by estimating
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
as a general low-rank matrix regression problem using the residuals from the first step, leveraging the fact that
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
’s and
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
’s are uncorrelated. For the low-rank setting, we maintain the joint update of both parameter matrices in our description to align with the convergence proof provided.
In contrast, for the sparse setting, we adopt this two-step method, enabling the direct application of existing accelerated algorithms for sparse vector regression.

Algorithm 1

Accelerated Projected Gradient Descent

Input :
Initial values
(\boldsymbol{B}^{(0)},\boldsymbol{A}^{(0)})
, step size
L^{(0)}
, and
\gamma>1
which is the multiplicative factor for searching for
L
satisfying a convergence condition.

/*
******* Initialization ******* */

1

Set initial search points
(\boldsymbol{P}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{Q}^{(1)})=(\boldsymbol{B}^{(0)},\boldsymbol{A}^{(0)})
, and
\alpha^{(1)}=1
which is used to generate weights for search points.

2

Set
k\leftarrow 1
.

3

repeat

/*
******* Estimate step size
L^{(k)}
to ensure convergence condition ******* */

4

Set:
\bar{L}\leftarrow L^{(k-1)}
.

5

Calculate:

6

p_{\bar{L}}(\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)})\leftarrow{\cal T}_{\lambda_{n}/\bar{L}}\big(\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)}-\frac{1}{\bar{L}}\nabla_{B}f_{n}(\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)})\big)
.

7

q_{\bar{L}}(\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)})\leftarrow\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)}-\frac{1}{\bar{L}}\nabla_{A}f_{n}(\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)})
.

8

repeat

9

Set:
\bar{L}\leftarrow\gamma\bar{L}
.

10

Calculate:

11

p_{\bar{L}}(\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)})\leftarrow{\cal T}_{\lambda_{n}/\bar{L}}\big(\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)}-\frac{1}{\bar{L}}\nabla_{B}f_{n}(\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)})\big)
.

12

q_{\bar{L}}(\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)})\leftarrow\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)}-\frac{1}{\bar{L}}\nabla_{A}f_{n}(\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)})
.

13

until

Condition (
14
) is satisfied
;

14

Set:
L^{(k)}\leftarrow\bar{L}
.

/*
******* Update iterates by projected gradient descent ******* */

15

Update approximates:

16

\boldsymbol{B}^{(k)}\leftarrow{\cal T}_{\lambda_{n}/L^{(k)}}\big(\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)}-\frac{1}{L^{(k)}}\nabla_{B}f_{n}(\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)})\big)
.

17

\boldsymbol{A}^{(k)}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)}-\frac{1}{L^{(k)}}\nabla_{A}f_{n}(\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)})
.

18

Update weighting sequence:
\alpha^{(k+1)}=0.5\times\left(1+\sqrt{1+4(\alpha^{(k)})^{2}}\right)
.

19

Update search points:
(\boldsymbol{P}^{(k+1)},\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k+1)})\leftarrow(\boldsymbol{B}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{A}^{(k)})+\frac{\alpha^{(k)}-1}{\alpha^{(k+1)}}\big((\boldsymbol{B}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{A}^{(k)})-(\boldsymbol{B}^{(k-1)},\boldsymbol{A}^{(k-1)})\big)
.

20

k\leftarrow k+1
.

21

until

\frac{\|\boldsymbol{B}^{(k)}-\boldsymbol{B}^{(k-1)}\|_{F}}{1+\|\boldsymbol{B}^{(k-1)}\|_{F}}+\frac{\|\boldsymbol{A}^{(k)}-\boldsymbol{A}^{(k-1)}\|_{F}}{1+\|\boldsymbol{A}^{(k-1)}\|_{F}}<\epsilon
;

Output :

\boldsymbol{A}^{(k)}
and
\boldsymbol{B}^{(k)}

Accelerated algorithm for sparse coefficient matrix
\boldsymbol{B}
.

Define
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}:=\widehat{\mathbb{F}}(\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbb{F}})^{-1}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{\top}
as the projection matrix, and let
\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{y}:=(\boldsymbol{I}_{n}-\widehat{\mathbb{P}})\boldsymbol{y}
represent the residuals of the response vector
\boldsymbol{y}
after projection onto the column space of
\widehat{\mathbb{F}}
, which contains vectorized factors. Given that
\widehat{\mathbb{U}}=(\boldsymbol{I}_{n}-\widehat{\mathbb{P}})\mathbb{X}
results in
\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbb{U}}=\boldsymbol{0}
, the solution to (
4
) can be straightforwardly determined as:

\displaystyle\vect(\widehat{}\boldsymbol{A})=\big(\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}\big)^{-1}\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{y},

\displaystyle\vect(\widehat{}\boldsymbol{B})\in\argmin_{\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}p_{2}}}\left\{\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\widetilde{y}_{i}-\left\langle\boldsymbol{B},\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{i}\right\rangle\right\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda_{n}\|\vect(\boldsymbol{B})\|_{1}\right\}.

(16)

Nesterov’s accelerated gradient algorithms
(
Simon et al., 2013
;
Yang & Zou, 2015
;
Yu et al., 2015
)
can be effectively utilized to solve convex problem (
16
). For a comprehensive review of additional computational methods for sparsity, refer to Chapter 3.5 in
Fan, Li, Zhang & Zou (2020)
.

3
Theoretical Results

3.1
Analysis of the Pre-Trained Projection

We start with some assumptions that are necessary for our theoretical development.

Assumption 5
.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
,
i\in[n]
are mean zero, and thus
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
are mean zero. Throughout this paper, we assume that
\{\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
are observed, and latent
\{(\boldsymbol{F}_{i},\boldsymbol{U}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
are i.i.d. copies of
(\boldsymbol{F},\boldsymbol{U})
.

Additionally, we adhere to Assumptions
19
and
20
outlined in the appendix. These are standard assumptions in factor models, as discussed in
Chen & Fan (2021)
. They are presented in the appendix due to constraints on page length.

Identification issue is inherent with latent factor models: for any invertible matrices
\boldsymbol{O}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}\times k_{1}}
and
\boldsymbol{O}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}\times k_{2}}
, the triples
(\boldsymbol{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{F}_{i},\boldsymbol{C}^{*})
and
(\boldsymbol{R}^{*}\boldsymbol{O}_{1},\boldsymbol{O}_{1}^{-1}\boldsymbol{F}_{i}\boldsymbol{O}_{2}^{-1},\boldsymbol{O}_{2}\boldsymbol{C}^{*})
are equivalent under the MFM model (
1
).
The following assumptions are commonly used to separate
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
and identify one targeting value of
(\boldsymbol{R}^{*},\boldsymbol{C}^{*})
.

Assumption 6
.

(a)

There exist universal constants
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
,
C_{1}
, and
C_{2}
such that

\displaystyle c_{1}\leq\lambda_{min}\big(\frac{1}{p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{R}^{*}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{R}^{*}}\big)\leq\lambda_{max}\big(\frac{1}{p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{R}^{*}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{R}^{*}}\big)\leq C_{1},

\displaystyle c_{2}\leq\lambda_{min}\big(\frac{1}{p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{C}^{*}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{C}^{*}}\big)\leq\lambda_{max}\big(\frac{1}{p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{C}^{*}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{C}^{*}\big)\leq C_{2}.

(b)

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{p_{1}}\boldsymbol{F}_{i}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{R}^{*}}{\boldsymbol{R}^{*}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{F}_{i}\right]=\boldsymbol{I}_{k_{2}}
, and
\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{p_{2}}\boldsymbol{F}_{i}{\boldsymbol{C}^{*}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{C}^{*}}\boldsymbol{F}_{i}^{\top}\right]=\boldsymbol{I}_{k_{1}}
.

The following proposition shows that the pre-trained projection matrices
\boldsymbol{W}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{W}_{2}
proposed in Section
2.1
satisfies Definition
4
as diversified projection matrices.

Proposition 7
.

Suppose
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
,
i\in[n]
are i.i.d. copies of
\boldsymbol{X}
defined in the MFM (
1
). Under Assumption
5
,
6
, and
19
in the appendix, there exist universal constants
c_{1},c_{2},
and
c_{3}
such that the matrices
\boldsymbol{H}_{1}=\frac{1}{p_{1}}\boldsymbol{W}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{H}_{2}=\frac{1}{p_{2}}\boldsymbol{W}_{2}^{\top}\boldsymbol{C}^{*}
satisfy

c_{1}-c_{2}\left(\sqrt{t(k_{1}\vee k_{2})}\sqrt{\frac{\log k_{j}+t}{n}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{j}}}\right)\leq\nu_{min}\big(\boldsymbol{H}_{j}\big)\leq\nu_{max}\big(\boldsymbol{H}_{j}\big)\leq c_{3}

for both
j=1,2
with probability at least
1-11e^{-t}
.

Proposition
7
indicates that when
n^{\prime}\gg k_{1}k_{2}+t
and
p_{1},p_{2}\gg 1
, the conditions of diversified projection matrices in Definition
4
are satisfied by the pre-trained projection matrices we proposed in Section
2.1
with
\nu_{\min}(\boldsymbol{H}_{j})\asymp 1
, for
j=1,2
.
In particular, by taking
t=\log n
, this condition holds with probability at least
1-11/n
.
With that,
the convergence rate of pre-trained estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{i}
to the true
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
up to transformation matrices is given below.

Proposition 8
.

Suppose that assumptions in Proposition
7
and Assumption
20
(a) hold.
Invoking Proposition
7
with
t=\log n
, we have, as
p_{1}\wedge p_{2}\rightarrow\infty
and
n
is finite or
n\rightarrow\infty
,
for any
i\in[n]
,

\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{H}_{1}\boldsymbol{F}_{i}\boldsymbol{H}_{2}^{\top}\|_{2}={\mathcal{O}_{p}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{1}p_{2}}}\right).

Remark 1
.

The optimality of the rate presented in Proposition
8
can be understood by considering an oracle scenario where
\boldsymbol{R}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{C}^{*}
are known.
This scenario reduces the problem to estimating
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
in a linear regression framework by vectorizing the matrix factor model:

\vect(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{*}\vect(\boldsymbol{F}_{i})+\vect(\boldsymbol{U}_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}p_{2}}.

Here,
\vect(\boldsymbol{F}_{i})
, with dimension
k_{1}k_{2}
, acts as the unknown “parameter” vector to be estimated. Standard OLS analysis indicates that the convergence rate of this estimator is
1/\sqrt{p_{1}p_{2}}
, aligning with the rate achieved in Proposition
8
.

3.2
Properties of the Block-Wise Averaged Estimators

Proposition
26
in Section
D.2
of the appendix establishes the asymptotic normality of
\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
obtained by least squares in (
9
).
Next we establish the asymptotic normality of the block-wise averaged estimator (
11
) of the loading matrices and element-wise convergence rate of the estimator (
12
) of the idiosyncratic component.
All proofs are relegated to Section
D
in the appendix.

Theorem 9
.

Under the condition of Proposition
26
and
{n}\ll{p_{1}p_{2}}\cdot(p_{1}\wedge p_{2})
, we have

\sqrt{np_{2}k_{2}}\left(\widehat{}\boldsymbol{R}-\mathring{c}_{s}\mathring{\boldsymbol{R}}\right)\xrightarrow{\ d\ }{\cal M}{\cal N}\big(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{R,p},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{R,k}\big)

(17)

and

\sqrt{np_{1}k_{1}}\left(\widehat{}\boldsymbol{C}-\mathring{r}_{s}\mathring{\boldsymbol{C}}\right)\xrightarrow{\ d\ }{\cal M}{\cal N}\big(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{C,p},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{C,k}\big).

(18)

where
\mathring{c}:=\sum_{i=1}^{p_{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{2}}\mathring{c}_{ij}/({p_{2}k_{2}})
,
\mathring{r}:=\sum_{i=1}^{p_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{1}}\mathring{r}_{ij}/({p_{1}k_{1}})
are scalars,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{R}}
and
\mathring{\boldsymbol{C}}
are rotated truth defined in (
10
), and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{R,p}
,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{C,q}
,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{R,k}
,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{C,k}
are covariance matrices of dimension
p_{1}\times p_{1}
,
p_{2}\times p_{2}
,
k_{1}\times k_{1}
, and
k_{2}\times k_{2}
, respectively, with finite entries.

Remark 2
.

The optimality of the rate for
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{R}
presented in Theorem
9
can be interpreted by considering an oracle scenario where
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{C}^{*}
are known. This scenario reduces the problem to estimating
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
using a linear regression model by stacking the matrix factor model along the second axis:

[\boldsymbol{X}_{1}\;\cdots\;\boldsymbol{X}_{n}]=\boldsymbol{R}\;[\boldsymbol{F}_{1}\boldsymbol{C}^{*\top}\;\cdots\;\boldsymbol{F}_{n}\boldsymbol{C}^{*\top}]+[\boldsymbol{E}_{1}\;\cdots\;\boldsymbol{E}_{n}].

In this model,
\boldsymbol{R}
of dimension
p_{1}\times k_{1}
, is treated as the unknown “parameter” vector. According to standard OLS analysis, the element-wise convergence rate of this estimator is
1/\sqrt{np_{2}}
, which is consistent with the rate determined in Theorem
9
.

Theorem 10
.

Under the same condition of Theorem
9
, we have, for each
i\in[n]
,
j\in[p_{1}]
, and
k\in[p_{2}]
, that

\left|\widehat{u}_{i,jk}-{u}_{i,jk}\right|={\mathcal{O}_{p}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{np_{1}}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{np_{2}}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{1}p_{2}}}\right),

(19)

where
\widehat{u}_{i,jk}
and
{u}_{i,jk}
are the
(j,k)
-th element of
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
, respectively.

Remark 3
.

The three components that shows up in the convergence rate in Theorem
10
comes from the errors in estimating
\boldsymbol{C}^{*}
,
\boldsymbol{R}^{*}
, and
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
, respectively.

3.3
Statistical Convergence of FAMAR Coefficients

We focus our theoretical analysis on latent FAMAR (
1
) where
(\boldsymbol{F}_{i},\boldsymbol{U}_{i})
are not directly observable.
Theoretical results for observable FAMAR are provided in Section
E
for low-rank
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
and in Section
G
for overall sparse
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
in the supplemental material.

3.3.1
FAMAR with Low-Rank
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}

To study the property of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{A}}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}
obtained by solving (
3
), we introduce the adjusted-restricted strong convexity condition (adjusted-RSC), which is a modification of the strong convexity condition (RSC)
(
Negahban & Wainwright, 2011
)
.

Definition 11

(Adjusted-restricted strong convexity)
.

We let
\Pi_{B^{\perp}}
denote the projection operator onto the matrix subspace
\{\boldsymbol{M}\;|\;{\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{*}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{M}=\boldsymbol{0}\text{ or }{\boldsymbol{M}}{\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{*}}=\boldsymbol{0}\}
where
\boldsymbol{V}_{1}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{V}_{2}^{*}
are the left and right singular space of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
respectively.
Any
\Delta_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times p_{2}}
can be decomposed as

\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\prime\prime}_{B}=\Pi_{B^{\perp}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}),\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\prime}_{B}=\boldsymbol{\Delta}-\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{\prime\prime}_{B}.

(20)

Define a set in
\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}\times k_{2}}\times\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times p_{2}}
as

\mathcal{C}:=\left\{\boldsymbol{\Delta}=(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{A},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{B})\mid\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}\times k_{2}},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times p_{2}},\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{B}^{\prime\prime}\|_{*}\leq 3\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{B}^{\prime}\|_{*}+\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{A}\|_{*}\right\}.

(21)

We say that the operator
\mathcal{G}_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}\times k_{2}}\times\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times p_{2}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfies adjusted-RSC over the set
\mathcal{C}
if there exists some scalar-valued
\kappa(\mathcal{G}_{n})>0
such that

\frac{1}{2n}\|\mathcal{G}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{A},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{B})\|^{2}\geq\kappa(\mathcal{G}_{n})\left(\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{A}\|_{F}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{B}\|_{F}^{2}\right)\ \text{ for all }(\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{A},\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{B})\in\mathcal{C}.

Theorem 12
.

Suppose that the projection matrices
\boldsymbol{W}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{W}_{2}
are generated by the pre-trained projection in Section
2.1
with
n^{\prime}\gg\log(p_{1}\vee p_{2})
,
n\ll p_{1}^{2}p_{2}^{2}
. Then, under the same condition of Proposition
26
, the solutions
(\widehat{}\boldsymbol{A},\widehat{}\boldsymbol{B})
to the optimization problem (
3
) satisfy

\left\|\boldsymbol{H}_{1}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{H}_{2}-\boldsymbol{A}^{*}\right\|_{F}={\mathcal{O}_{p}}\left(\frac{\rho}{\sqrt{np_{1}p_{2}}}\right),

(22)

where
\rho:=\|\boldsymbol{A}^{*}\|_{F}
is the Frobenious norm of the true
k_{1}\times k_{2}
coefficient matrix. In addition, suppose that there exists a positive constant
C_{0}
such that
\frac{p_{1}\vee p_{2}}{n}<C_{0}
, and
\mathcal{G}_{n}
satisfies adjusted-RSC with constant
\kappa(\mathcal{G}_{n})
over the set
\mathcal{C}
, where
\kappa(\mathcal{G}_{n})>2C_{1}(C_{0}+1)
for some constant
C_{1}
.
Then we have

\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{F}\leq(2\kappa(\mathcal{G}_{n})^{-1})\cdot\lambda_{n}\cdot\big(2\sqrt{k_{1}\wedge k_{2}}+6\sqrt{2\operatorname{rank}(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})}\big),

(23)

when the tuning parameter
\lambda_{n}
is chosen such that

\lambda_{n}\geq 2\|\hbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 2.15277pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$\mathcal{G}$\kern 0.0pt}}}_{n}^{*}(\bar{}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\|_{2}/n
, where
\hbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 2.15277pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$\mathcal{G}$\kern 0.0pt}}}_{n}^{*}({\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\cdot\operatorname{diag}\big(\boldsymbol{H}_{1}^{-1}\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{H}_{2}^{\top})^{-1},\;\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{i}\big)
and
\hbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 2.15277pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$\varepsilon$\kern 0.0pt}}}_{i}=y_{i}-\left\langle\boldsymbol{H}_{1}^{-1}\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{H}_{2}^{\top})^{-1},\boldsymbol{A}^{*}\right\rangle-\left\langle\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{i},\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\right\rangle
.

Remark 4
.

In FAMAR model (
1
), the signal strength from the factors
\langle\boldsymbol{A}^{*},\boldsymbol{F}_{i}\rangle
should be comparable to the signal strength from the idiosyncratic component
\langle\boldsymbol{B}^{*},\boldsymbol{U}_{i}\rangle
.
Since the predictors
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}\times k_{2}}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times p_{2}}
both have bounded entries, but differ in dimensions, it is reasonable to expect
\|\boldsymbol{A}^{*}\|_{F}\asymp\mathcal{O}\left(p_{1}p_{2}\right)
in the low-rank setting when
\|\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{F}\asymp\mathcal{O}\left(p_{1}p_{2}\right)
.
This aligns with the matrix regression model (
2
) in Example 2, where the size of each element in
\boldsymbol{A}^{*}
inherently has order
\mathcal{O}\left(p_{1}p_{2}\right)
, given the dimensions of
\boldsymbol{R}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times k_{1}}
,
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times p_{2}}
, and
\boldsymbol{C}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{2}\times k_{2}}
.
Consequently, the convergence rate of
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{A}
in (
22
) is interpreted for the relative error

\frac{\left\|\boldsymbol{H}_{1}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{H}_{2}-\boldsymbol{A}^{*}\right\|_{F}}{\|\boldsymbol{A}^{*}\|_{F}}={\mathcal{O}_{p}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{np_{1}p_{2}}}\right).

This contrasts sharply with factor-augmented sparse vector or matrix regression models where
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
is sparsely supported on a set
S
,
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\right\rVert_{F}\asymp\mathcal{O}\left(\left\lvert S\right\rvert\right)
, and
\|\boldsymbol{A}^{*}\|_{F}\asymp\mathcal{O}\left(k_{1}k_{2}\right)
.

The convergence rate of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}
described in Theorem
12
can be further clarified when FAMAR reduces to matrix regression with MFM covariates (
2
), or equivalently
\boldsymbol{A}^{*}=\boldsymbol{R}^{\top}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\boldsymbol{C}
, and
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
is a sub-Gaussian random vector. This scenario is elaborated in the subsequent proposition.

Proposition 13
.

Under FAMAR model (
1
) with
\boldsymbol{A}^{*}=\boldsymbol{R}^{\top}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\boldsymbol{C}
and the conditions of Theorem
12
, if
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{i}[\boldsymbol{F}_{i}\;\boldsymbol{U}_{i}]]=\boldsymbol{0}
, and
\varepsilon_{i}
is
\sigma
-sub-Gaussian,
then when the tuning parameter
\lambda_{n}
is chosen such that
\lambda_{n}\asymp\sqrt{\log(p_{1}+p_{2})/n}+(\log n/n)^{1/4}\sqrt{p_{1}p_{2}/n}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{F}=\mathcal{O}_{p}\big[\big(2\sqrt{k_{1}\wedge k_{2}}

\displaystyle+6\sqrt{2\operatorname{rank}(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})}\big)\big(\sqrt{\frac{\log(k_{1}+k_{2}+p_{1}+p_{2})}{n}}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right)^{1/4}\sqrt{(k_{1}\wedge k_{2})k_{1}k_{2}}+\frac{(\log n)^{1/4}}{n^{3/4}}\sqrt{p_{1}p_{2}}\big)\big].

In particular, since
k_{1}\asymp k_{2}\asymp\rank(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\asymp 1
, the result is simplified to

\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{F}={\mathcal{O}_{p}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log(p_{1}+p_{2})}{n}}+\frac{(\log n)^{1/4}\sqrt{p_{1}p_{2}}}{n^{3/4}}\right).

Remark 5
.

The two primary components contributing to the error bound rate for
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{B}
are: (i) the interaction between regression noise
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
and the true covariates
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
’s and
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
’s, quantified as
\|n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\;{\rm blockdiag}[\boldsymbol{F}_{i}\;\boldsymbol{U}_{i}]\|_{2}
, and (ii) the interaction of regression noise
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
with the estimation errors of
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
’s, quantified as
\|n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}(\widehat{}\boldsymbol{U}_{i}-\boldsymbol{U}_{i})\|_{2}
.

3.3.2
FAMAR with Sparse
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}

When the true
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
exhibits certain sparse structures, such as global, row- or column-wise sparsity, the methodologies and theoretical frameworks developed for high-dimensional vector regression with sparse or group sparse structures can be effectively adapted to the FAMAR model with sparse
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
. We have established theoretical results for overall sparsity using LASSO. Analogous results could be developed for overall sparsity employing non-concave penalties like SCAD and MCP, or for group sparsity, leveraging the relevant literature, e.g.
Yuan & Lin (2006)
;
Obozinski et al. (2011)
;
Hastie et al. (2015)
.
We leave these developments for future research to maintain focus and adhere to page limits.

To study the property of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{A}}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}
obtained by solving (
4
), we consider the vectorized version with the restricted strong convexity condition (RSC) in Assumption
14
and adapt the proofs in
Fan, Ke & Wang (2020)
.
The analysis on
\boldsymbol{A}
is exactly the same as Theorem
12
based on the fact that
\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbb{U}}=\boldsymbol{0}
, we include it in the following theorem for completeness.
The consistency of
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{B}
is established in Theorem
15
.

Assumption 14
.

We denote
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}:=(\vect(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top},\vect(\boldsymbol{A}^{*})^{\top})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}p_{2}+K_{1}K_{2}}
, and
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}:=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{[p_{1}p_{2}]}=\vect(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})
and denote
S:=\supp(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})
,
S_{1}=\supp(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})
, and
S_{2}=[p_{1}p_{2}+K_{1}K_{2}]\setminus S
.
Let
\boldsymbol{W}:=[\mathbb{U}\;\;\mathbb{F}]
represent the
n\times(p_{1}p_{2}+k_{1}k_{2})
true covariate matrix, we assume that

(a)

(Restricted strong convexity) There exist
\kappa_{2}>\kappa_{\infty}>0
such that
\|n(\boldsymbol{W}_{S}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}_{S})^{-1}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{2\kappa_{\infty}}
and
\|n(\boldsymbol{W}_{S}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}_{S})^{-1}\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{2\kappa_{2}}
, where
\boldsymbol{W}_{S}
is the matrix of columns of
\boldsymbol{W}
supported on
S
;

(b)

(Irrepresentable condition)
\|\boldsymbol{W}_{S_{2}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}_{S}(\boldsymbol{W}_{S}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}_{S})^{-1}\|_{\infty}\leq 1-\tau
for some
\tau\in(0,1)
.

Theorem 15
.

The solutions
(\widehat{}\boldsymbol{A},\widehat{}\boldsymbol{B})
to the optimization problem (
4
) satisfy

\left\|\boldsymbol{H}_{1}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{H}_{2}-\boldsymbol{A}^{*}\right\|_{F}={\mathcal{O}_{p}}\left(\frac{\rho}{\sqrt{np_{1}p_{2}}}\right),

where
\rho:=\|\boldsymbol{A}^{*}\|_{F}
, if the same conditions hold for the factors as in Theorem
12
. In addition,
suppose Assumption
14
and
34
hold.
Define

\hbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 2.15277pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$\delta$\kern 0.0pt}}}=\left\lVert n^{-1}\hbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 2.15277pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$\boldsymbol{W}$\kern 0.0pt}}}_{S}^{\top}\left(\hbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 2.15277pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$\boldsymbol{W}$\kern 0.0pt}}}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{y}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty},

let
\hbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 2.15277pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$\boldsymbol{W}$\kern 0.0pt}}}:=[\hbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 2.15277pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$\mathbb{U}$\kern 0.0pt}}}\;\;\hbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 2.15277pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$\mathbb{F}$\kern 0.0pt}}}]
represent the
n\times(p_{1}p_{2}+k_{1}k_{2})
rotated estimated covariate matrix and
\hbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 2.15277pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$\boldsymbol{W}$\kern 0.0pt}}}_{S}
represent sub-matrix of

\boldsymbol{W}

supported on
S
.
If
\lambda>\frac{14\hbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 1.50694pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$\delta$\kern 0.0pt}}}}{\tau}
, then we have
\supp(\widehat{}\boldsymbol{B})\subseteq\supp(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})
and

\big\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}-{\boldsymbol{B}}^{*}\big\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{12\lambda}{5\kappa_{\infty}},\\
\quad\big\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}-{\boldsymbol{B}}^{*}\big\|_{2}\leq\frac{4\lambda\sqrt{|S|}}{\kappa_{2}},\\
\quad\big\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}-{\boldsymbol{B}}^{*}\big\|_{1}\leq\frac{12\lambda|S|}{5\kappa_{\infty}}.

The proof is presented in Section
H
in the appendix.
By taking
\lambda\asymp\hbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 2.15277pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$\delta$\kern 0.0pt}}}
, we have

\|\widehat{}\boldsymbol{B}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{\infty}/\hbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 2.15277pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$\delta$\kern 0.0pt}}}={\mathcal{O}_{p}}\left(1\right),\quad\|\widehat{}\boldsymbol{B}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{2}/\hbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 2.15277pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$\delta$\kern 0.0pt}}}={\mathcal{O}_{p}}\left(\sqrt{|S|}\right),\quad\text{and}\quad\|\widehat{}\boldsymbol{B}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{1}/\hbox{\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt\kern 2.15277pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$\delta$\kern 0.0pt}}}={\mathcal{O}_{p}}\left(|S|\right).

The convergence rate of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}
described from Theorem
15
can be further clarified when FAMAR reduces to matrix regression with MFM covariates (
2
), that is
\boldsymbol{A}^{*}=\boldsymbol{R}^{\top}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\boldsymbol{C}
, and
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
is a sub-Gaussian random vector. This scenario is elaborated in the subsequent corollary.

Corollary 16
.

Suppose the conditions for Theorem
15
hold. In addition, we assume that the true matrix regression model is
y_{i}=\left\langle\boldsymbol{X}_{i},\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\right\rangle+\varepsilon_{i}
, i.e.
\boldsymbol{A}^{*}=\boldsymbol{R}^{*\top}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\boldsymbol{C}^{*}
, and that
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{i}(\boldsymbol{F}_{i}\ \boldsymbol{U}_{i})]=0
and
\varepsilon_{i}
is
\sigma
-sub-Gaussian. Then

\|\widehat{}\boldsymbol{B}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{\infty}={\mathcal{O}_{p}}\left(\frac{|S|\log(p_{1}p_{2})}{n}+|S|\sqrt{\frac{\log p_{1}p_{2}}{n}}\left(\frac{p_{1}}{p_{2}}\vee\frac{p_{2}}{p_{1}}\right)^{1/2}\right).

(24)

Remark 6
.

When
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
is a balanced matrix, that is,
p_{1}\asymp p_{2}
, error bound (
24
) can be further reduce to

\|\widehat{}\boldsymbol{B}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{\infty}={\mathcal{O}_{p}}\left(|S|\sqrt{\frac{\log p_{1}p_{2}}{n}}\right),

which is optimal for vector sparse regression for vectorized
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
.

4
Simulations

We study the finite-sample performance of the proposed FAMAR through simulations.

FAMAR Matrix Loading’s Asymptotically Normality.

We test the asymptotic normality for the estimated loading matrices
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{R}
and
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{C}
according to Theorem
9
.
To that end, we conduct experiments as follows:
n=1000,\ \ n^{\prime}=500,\ k_{1}=3,\ k_{2}=2,\ \rank(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})=2,\ p_{1}=20,\ p_{2}=30
. The true loadings
\boldsymbol{R}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{C}^{*}
are generated such that their elements are i.i.d. from
{\cal N}(2,4)
. The true factors
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
and residuals
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
have their elements i.i.d. from
{\cal N}(0,1)
and
{\cal N}(0,0.04)
, respectively. The experiments are repeated 10,000 times, and in each repetition, we compute
\sqrt{np_{2}k_{2}}\big(\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}-\mathring{c}_{s}\mathring{\boldsymbol{R}}\big)
and
\sqrt{np_{1}k_{1}}\big(\widehat{\boldsymbol{C}}-\mathring{r}_{s}\mathring{\boldsymbol{C}}\big)
.
For each elements
(j,k)
,
j\in[p_{1}]
,
k\in[k_{1}]
(resp.
(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})
,
j^{\prime}\in[p_{2}]
,
k^{\prime}\in[k_{2}]
), we get 10,000 realizations of
\sqrt{np_{2}k_{2}}\big(\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}-\mathring{c}_{s}\mathring{\boldsymbol{R}}\big)_{jk}
(resp.
\sqrt{np_{1}k_{1}}\big(\widehat{\boldsymbol{C}}-\mathring{r}_{s}\mathring{\boldsymbol{C}}\big)_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}
). Let
\widehat{s}_{jk}
(resp.
\widehat{s}_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}^{\prime}
) be the sample standard deviation of the sequence.
According to Theorem
9
,
\sqrt{np_{2}k_{2}}\big(\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}-\mathring{c}_{s}\mathring{\boldsymbol{R}}\big)_{jk}/\widehat{s}_{jk}
and
\sqrt{np_{1}k_{1}}\big(\widehat{\boldsymbol{C}}-\mathring{r}_{s}\mathring{\boldsymbol{C}}\big)_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}/\widehat{s}_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}^{\prime}
should distribute closely to standard Gaussian.
This result is validated empirically by histograms for all the elements of the matrices in Appendix
I.2
.
Figure
1
showcases histograms of four randomly picked elements.

(a)

\sqrt{np_{2}k_{2}}\big(\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}-c_{s}\mathring{\boldsymbol{R}}\big)_{10,1}/\widehat{s}_{10,1}

(b)

\sqrt{np_{2}k_{2}}\big(\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}-c_{s}\mathring{\boldsymbol{R}}\big)_{19,3}/\widehat{s}_{19,3}

(c)

\sqrt{np_{1}k_{1}}\big(\widehat{\boldsymbol{C}}-r_{s}\mathring{\boldsymbol{C}}\big)_{3,1}/\widehat{s}_{3,1}^{\prime}

(d)

\sqrt{np_{1}k_{1}}\big(\widehat{\boldsymbol{C}}-r_{s}\mathring{\boldsymbol{C}}\big)_{13,2}/\widehat{s}_{13,2}^{\prime}

Figure 1
:
Elementwise normality check for the estimated loadings
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{R}
and
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{C}
according to Theorem
9
. The blue histograms are empirical densities, and the orange lines are the standard Gaussian density functions.

Next, we investigate the accuracy of the estimated factors, idiosyncratic variable, and FAMAR model coefficients. All experiments are repeated 100 times to reduce the effect of randomness.
The simulations are carried out under two settings of parameters:

Setting
I.

Fix
n=1000,\ n_{new}=1000,\ n^{\prime}=500,\ k_{1}=k_{2}=2,\ \rank(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})=2,
and change
\ p_{1}=p_{2}
in
\{20,30,40,50,60,70,80,90,100\}
.

Setting
II.

Fix
p_{1}=80,\ p_{2}=50,\ n_{new}=1000,\ n^{\prime}=500,\ k_{1}=2,\ k_{2}=4,\ \operatorname{rank}(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})=2,
and change
n
in
\{500,1000,1500,2000,2500,3000,3500,4000,4500,5000\}
.

More settings of simulation exploration are available in Appendix
I
.
For all cases, the true loading matrices
\boldsymbol{R}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{C}^{*}
are generated element-wise from
{\cal N}(2,4)
. The true factors
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
and residuals
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
are generated such that each element is drawn from
{\cal N}(0,1)
and
{\cal N}(0,0.04)
, respectively. Thereafter, the
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
are generated by the matrix factor model
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}=\boldsymbol{R}^{*}\boldsymbol{F}_{i}(\boldsymbol{C}^{*})^{\top}+\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
.
For
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
, we first generate a
p_{1}\times p_{2}
matrix, with its individual elements sampled from
{\cal N}(0.5,0.25)
, and then we take its best low-rank (
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
) approximation by SVD. The true response variables are defined by
y_{i}=\left\langle\boldsymbol{A}^{*},\boldsymbol{F}_{i}\right\rangle+\left\langle\boldsymbol{B}^{*},\boldsymbol{U}_{i}\right\rangle+\varepsilon_{i}
, where
\boldsymbol{A}^{*}:=0.5\cdot(\boldsymbol{R}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\boldsymbol{C}^{*}
and
\varepsilon_{i}
are drawn from
{\cal N}(0,1)
.
By setting the variance of loading matrices much higher than that of the residual, we generate
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
with highly correlated rows and columns.
The experiments are repeated 100 times to reduce the impact of randomness. The tuning parameter
\lambda
for the nuclear norm penalty is chosen by cross-validation.

FAMAR Matrix Factor and Idiosyncratic Estimation.

Figures
2
and
3
plot the performance of the proposed algorithm in estimating
\boldsymbol{F}
and
\boldsymbol{U}
under
Setting I.
and
Setting II.
, respectively.
The estimation errors are all very small and decrease as either
n
or
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
increase, except that the error for
\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}
is not affected by increasing number of
n
as it uses
n^{\prime}
pre-training sample size.
Those observations are consistent with our theoretical results. The comparison of the estimation on
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{U}
with and without the block-averaging method showcases the privilege of the averaging algorithm.

(\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}})

and increasing

. For factors, we report the relative error
{\|\mathbb{F}(\boldsymbol{H}_{2}\otimes\boldsymbol{H}_{1})^{\top}-\widehat{\mathbb{F}}\|_{F}}/{\|\mathbb{F}(\boldsymbol{H}_{2}\otimes\boldsymbol{H}_{1})^{\top}\|_{F}}
, where
\mathbb{F}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k_{1}k_{2}}
is the matrix of vectorized true factors, and
\widehat{\mathbb{F}}
is the estimation. For simplicity of notation, we omit the rotation matrix
\boldsymbol{H}_{2}\otimes\boldsymbol{H}_{1}
in the figure label. Similar relative errors are reported for the estimation of idiosyncratic components, also comparing the performance with and without the block-average technique. The solid lines are the means among the 100 repetitions, the shadows show the standard deviations, and the vertical lines present the quartiles (which are too small to be visible on the graphs).

is not affected by increasing number of
n
as it uses
n^{\prime}
pre-training sample size, which is consistent with Theorem
8
.

FAMAR Coefficient Estimation and Prediction.

Figures
4
and
5
compare our proposed FAMAR with two current benchmarks, namely, the vanilla matrix regression model (
2
) with nuclear penalty on
\boldsymbol{B}
, and the oracle factor augmented regression model (
1
) with observed
\boldsymbol{F}
and
\boldsymbol{U}
, under
Setting I.
and
Setting II.
, respectively.
We could clearly see that our proposed FAMAR estimation tracks the performances of the oracle factor augmented regression closely in almost all tasks.
Our proposed method outperforms the existing matrix regression with nuclear norm penalty in both estimation of coefficient
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
and prediction of new response
y
.
Additionally, by generating a low-rank
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{B}
, as illustrated in the lower-right plots of Figures
4
and
5
, FAMAR offers improved interpretability and stability compared to direct regression on
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
’s. When the covariates
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
are highly correlated, applying low-rank regression directly to
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
’s tends to overestimate the rank due to their dependency structure, which hampers the effectiveness of regularization and the interpretability of the model.

The superior performance of FAMAR stems from two key advantages. Firstly, since the space spanned by
(\boldsymbol{F}_{i},\boldsymbol{U}_{i})
is identical to that spanned by
(\boldsymbol{F}_{i},\boldsymbol{X}_{i})
, FAMAR facilitates feature augmentation with
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
, effectively leveraging latent information. Secondly, the factor model decorrelates the covariance matrix, resulting in more stable and interpretable coefficient estimators.

:

Fix
n=1000
, increase
p_{1}=p_{2}
from
20
to
100
. The blue, orange, and green lines correspond to running the regression with true
(\boldsymbol{F},\boldsymbol{U})
, estimated
(\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}})
, and
\boldsymbol{X}
, respectively.
The solid lines are the means over 100 repetitions, the shadows show the standard deviation, and the vertical lines present the quartiles. The lower-right figure shows the rank of
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{B}
with
\lambda
chosen by cross-validation under different models.

:

(p_{1},p_{2})=(70,50)
, increase
n
from
500
to
4000
. The blue, orange, and green lines correspond to doing the regression with true
(\boldsymbol{F},\boldsymbol{U})
, estimated
(\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}})
, and
\boldsymbol{X}
, respectively. The solid lines are the means over 100 repetitions, the shadows show the standard deviation, and the vertical lines present the quartiles.

5
Real Data Analysis

We demonstrate the advantages of FAMAR with an real application to the multinational macroeconomic indices collected from OECD
(
Chen et al., 2019
)
.
The dataset contains 10 quarterly macroeconomic indexes of 14 countries from 1990.Q4 to 2016.Q4 for 105 quarters.
By implementing the proposed FAMAR, we accomplish various empirical objectives, including the prediction of economic indicators, extraction of economic factors, and estimation of factor impacts.
Subsequent sections showcase the results.

Economic prediction.

Here we showcase one-step ahead prediction of individual countries GDP using all the economic indicators from all countries in the last period.
The covariate
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
is the
10
(index)
\times 14
(country) matrix in one quarter and the response
y_{i}
is the GDP of a country of interest in the next quarter.
We take the estimated factor dimension as
(k_{1},k_{2})=(4,4)
according to the analysis in
Chen & Fan (2021)
.

We use a rolling window to do a one-step ahead prediction on the GDP of the United States (USA), United Kingdom (GBR), France (FRA), and Germany (DEU).
We set the window width as 68, the first
n^{\prime}=32
samples of which are set to pre-train the projection matrices
\boldsymbol{W}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{W}_{2}
, while the following
n=36
samples are used to estimate the loading matrices
\boldsymbol{R}^{*}
,
\boldsymbol{C}^{*}
and the regression coefficient matrix
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
. In each window, the covariate matrices
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
are normalized by subtracting the mean and dividing the standard deviation of the training set. The response variable
y_{i}
is also demeaned by subtracting the sample mean of each training set.

The out-of-sample
R^{2}
of GDP prediction on some representative countries are reported in Table
1
.
It is clear that FAMAR outperforms the vanilla matrix regression with a nuclear-norm penalty with much higher out-of-sample
R^{2}
. Besides the comparison with the baseline, we also present predictions based only on the estimated factors
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{F}_{i}
or the estimated idiosyncratic components
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
, respectively, to showcase their individual contributions to the process. As expected, it appears that factors play a predominant role in prediction, whereas the idiosyncratic components capture comparatively less valuable information in the context. This aligns with our expectation, for factors should capture most of the main and common features, and the residuals are regarded as idiosyncratic components corresponding to each feature, which are less representative and may vary a lot across time.

Economic and Country Factors and Loadings.

Figure
6
shows the heatmaps for estimates of the row and column loading matrices for the
4\times 4
factor model.
The loading matrices are normalized so that the
\ell_{2}
norm of each column is one. They are also varimax-rotated to reveal a clear structure.
From Figure
6
(a) of the row factor loading matrix
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{R}
, we observed clearly that the rotated row factor 1-4 loaded heavily on central/western European, north American, Northern European, and Oceania countries, respectively.
Accordingly, the four rotated row factors can be interpreted as the Central/Western European (C/W-EU), North American (NA), Northern European (N-EU), and Oceanian (OC) factors, mostly following geographical partitions.
In a similar fashion, the four rotated column factors are (i) Economic output and industrial production (GDP&P); (ii) Consumer Price Index (CPI); (iii) International Trade (IT); (iv) Interest Rates (IR). Again, the factors agree with common economic knowledge.

after varimax rotation

\widehat{}\boldsymbol{A}

’s after rotation in Figure
6

Factors Impacts.

To explore the impact of latent factors on the GDP of different countries, Figure
7
presents the heat map of
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{A}
over the period. The heatmaps of
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{A}
exhibit notable similarities across the four examined countries. A discernible pattern reveals several coordinates characterized by substantial magnitudes. Specifically, the four most significant coefficients, in terms of magnitude, correspond to the pairs: (IT, NA), (IR, NA), (IT, OC), and (CPI, C/W-EU). This implies that international trade and interest rates in North America, international trade in Oceania, and the CPI of Central and Western Europe play pivotal roles in forecasting the GDP of the countries.

6
Conclusion

We introduce Factor-Augmented Matrix Regression (FAMAR) as a powerful framework to tackle the growing complexity of matrix-variate data and its inherent high-dimensionality challenges. FAMAR comprises two pivotal algorithms, each designed to address distinct aspects of the problem. The first algorithm presents an innovative, non-iterative approach that efficiently estimates the factors and loadings within the matrix factor model. Leveraging advanced techniques such as pre-training, diverse projection, and block-wise averaging, it streamlines the estimation process. The second algorithm, equally crucial, offers an accelerated solution for nuclear-norm penalized matrix factor regression. Both algorithms are underpinned by robust statistical and numerical convergence guarantees, ensuring their reliability in practical applications.

Empirical assessments across a range of synthetic and real datasets substantiate the efficacy of FAMAR. Notably, it outperforms existing methods in terms of prediction accuracy, factor interpretability, and computational efficiency. FAMAR’s versatility and superior performance make it a valuable addition to the toolkit of researchers and practitioners working with matrix-variate data. Its potential applications extend across diverse domains, from economics and finance to machine learning and data mining, where handling high-dimensional matrix data efficiently and effectively is paramount. As we move forward in the era of big data, FAMAR stands as a testament to the ongoing innovation in statistical modeling and data analysis, promising new avenues for extracting valuable insights from complex datasets.

Appendix A
Notations

We use the notation
[N]
to refer to the positive integer set
\left\{1,\ldots,N\right\}
for
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.
For any matrix
\boldsymbol{X}
, we use
\boldsymbol{x}_{i\cdot}
,
\boldsymbol{x}_{j}
, and
x_{ij}
to refer to its
i
-th row,
j
-th column, and
ij
-th entry, respectively.
All vectors are column vectors and row vectors are written as
\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}
for any vector
\boldsymbol{x}
.
For any two scalers
p
,
q
, we denote
p\wedge q:=\min\{p,q\}
and
p\vee q:=\max\{p,q\}
.

We use
\vect(\boldsymbol{X})
as the vectorization of
\boldsymbol{X}
and use
\mathbb{X}
to denote a matrix whose rows are specifically vectorized matrices. That is, the
i
-th row of
\mathbb{X}
is
\vect(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})^{\top}
. For two matrices
\boldsymbol{X}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
and
\boldsymbol{X}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
,
\boldsymbol{X}_{1}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{pm\,\times\,qn}
is the Kronecker product. Note that
\vect(\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{B})=(\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{A})\vect(\boldsymbol{X})
.
We denote
\diag(\boldsymbol{X}_{1},\boldsymbol{X}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{(m+p)\times(n+q)}
as the block-diagonal matrix with
\boldsymbol{X}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{X}_{2}
being its diagonal blocks.

For any vector
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p})^{\top}
, we let
\|\boldsymbol{x}\|:=\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{2}=(\sum_{i=1}^{p}x_{i}^{2})^{1/2}
be the
\ell_{2}
-norm, and let
\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}|x_{i}|
be the
\ell_{1}
-norm.
Besides, we use the following matrix norms:

\ell_{2}
-norm
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{X}\right\rVert_{2}:=\sigma_{max}(\boldsymbol{X})
;
(2,1)
-norm
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{X}\right\rVert_{2,1}:=\underset{\left\lVert\boldsymbol{a}\right\rVert_{1}=1}{\max}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{a}\right\rVert_{2}=\max_{i}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{x}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}
;
Frobenius norm
\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{F}=(\sum_{i,j}x_{ij}^{2})^{1/2}
;
nuclear norm
\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{*}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\sigma_{i}(\boldsymbol{X})}
.
When
\boldsymbol{X}
is a square matrix, we denote by
\Tr\left(\boldsymbol{X}\right)
,
\lambda_{max}\left(\boldsymbol{X}\right)
, and
\lambda_{min}\left(\boldsymbol{X}\right)
the trace, maximum and minimum singular value of
\boldsymbol{X}
, respectively.
For two matrices of the same dimension, define the inner product
\left\langle\boldsymbol{X}_{1},\boldsymbol{X}_{2}\right\rangle=\Tr(\boldsymbol{X}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{2})
.

Appendix B
Computation

B.1
Explanation of the Accelerated Algorithm

To elucidate the accelerated algorithm, we first consider the vanilla gradient algorithm with the gradient step performed at the approximate solution
\{(\boldsymbol{A}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{B}^{(k)})\}
, which consists of a singular value thresholding (SVT) on the gradient step of estimating
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
and a regular update of
\boldsymbol{A}^{*}
.
The partial gradient of
f_{n}
with respect to
\boldsymbol{B}
is

\nabla_{B}f_{n}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})=-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(y_{i}-\left\langle\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{F}_{i}\right\rangle-\left\langle\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{U}_{i}\right\rangle\right)\boldsymbol{U}_{i}.

Both
\nabla_{A}f_{n}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})
and
\nabla_{B}f_{n}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})
are
L
-Lipschitz with given
\{(\boldsymbol{F}_{i},\boldsymbol{U}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
.
Given a prescribed precision level
\epsilon
and some initialization
\boldsymbol{A}^{(0)}
and
\boldsymbol{B}^{(0)}
, we can estimate
\boldsymbol{A}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
alternatively.
Given
\boldsymbol{A}^{(k-1)}
,
\boldsymbol{B}^{(k-1)}
and the step size
L^{(k)}
,
\boldsymbol{B}
can be updated by:

\boldsymbol{B}^{(k)}=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{B}}\left\{\frac{L^{(k)}}{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{B}-\left(\boldsymbol{B}^{(k-1)}-\frac{1}{L^{(k)}}\nabla_{B}f_{n}(\boldsymbol{A}^{(k-1)},\boldsymbol{B}^{(k-1)})\right)\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lambda_{n}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{B}\right\rVert_{*}\right\},

(25)

which admits an analytic solution

\boldsymbol{B}^{(k)}={\cal T}_{\lambda_{n}/L^{(k)}}\left(\boldsymbol{B}^{(k-1)}-\frac{1}{L^{(k)}}\nabla_{B}f_{n}(\boldsymbol{A}^{(k-1)},\boldsymbol{B}^{(k-1)})\right):=p_{L^{(k)}}(\boldsymbol{A}^{(k-1)},\boldsymbol{B}^{(k-1)}),

(26)

where
{\cal T}_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{C})
is an operator defined as
{\cal T}_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{C})=\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{D}_{\lambda}\boldsymbol{V}^{\top}
on a matrix
\boldsymbol{C}
with SVD
\boldsymbol{C}=\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{D}\boldsymbol{V}^{\top}
and
\boldsymbol{D}_{\lambda}
is diagonal with the soft thresholding
(d_{\lambda})_{ii}=\sign(d_{ii})\max\{0,\left\lvert d_{ii}\right\rvert-\lambda\}
.
Since
\boldsymbol{A}
is of low dimension, it can be updated directly by gradient descent:

\boldsymbol{A}^{(k)}=q_{L^{(k)}}(\boldsymbol{A}^{(k-1)},\boldsymbol{B}^{(k-1)}):=\boldsymbol{A}^{(k-1)}-\frac{1}{L^{(k)}}\nabla_{A}f_{n}(\boldsymbol{A}^{(k-1)},\boldsymbol{B}^{(k-1)}).

(27)

The accelerated algorithm performs the gradient step at the search point
\{(\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)})\}
.
So
\boldsymbol{A}^{(k)}
and
\boldsymbol{B}^{(k)}
are calculated according to equation (
26
) and (
27
) with
(\boldsymbol{A}^{(k-1)},\boldsymbol{B}^{(k-1)})
replaced by the search point
(\boldsymbol{Q}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{P}^{(k)})
.

B.2
Numerical Convergence

Lemma 17
.

Given
\boldsymbol{A}^{(k-1)}
,
\boldsymbol{B}^{(k-1)}
and the step size
L^{(k)}
,
\boldsymbol{B}
can be updated by:

\boldsymbol{B}^{(k)}=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{B}}\left\{\frac{L^{(k)}}{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{B}-\left(\boldsymbol{B}^{(k-1)}-\frac{1}{L^{(k)}}\nabla_{B}f_{n}(\boldsymbol{A}^{(k-1)},\boldsymbol{B}^{(k-1)})\right)\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lambda_{n}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{B}\right\rVert_{*}\right\},

(28)

or, equivalently,

\boldsymbol{B}^{(k)}=p_{L^{(k)}}(\boldsymbol{A}^{(k-1)},\boldsymbol{B}^{(k-1)}):={\cal T}_{\lambda_{n}/L^{(k)}}\left(\boldsymbol{B}^{(k-1)}-\frac{1}{L^{(k)}}\nabla_{B}f_{n}(\boldsymbol{A}^{(k-1)},\boldsymbol{B}^{(k-1)})\right),

(29)

where
{\cal T}_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{C})
is an operator defined as
{\cal T}_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{C})=\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{D}_{\lambda}\boldsymbol{V}^{\top}
on a matrix
\boldsymbol{C}
with SVD
\boldsymbol{C}=\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{D}\boldsymbol{V}^{\top}
and
\boldsymbol{D}_{\lambda}
is diagonal with
(d_{\lambda})_{ii}=\sign(d_{ii})\max\{0,\left\lvert d_{ii}\right\rvert-\lambda\}
.

Proof of Lemma
17
.

Lemma
17
can be prove by invoking Theorem 2.1 in
(
Cai et al. 2010
)
.
∎

Lemma 18
.

Let

p_{L}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}}\big\{H_{L,B}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime};\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})+\lambda_{n}\|\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}\|_{*}\big\},\quad q_{L}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{A}^{\prime}}H_{L,A}(\boldsymbol{A}^{\prime};\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}),

where

\displaystyle H_{L,A}(\boldsymbol{A}^{\prime};\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})

\displaystyle:=\left\langle\boldsymbol{A}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{A},\nabla_{A}f_{n}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})\right\rangle+\frac{L}{2}\|\boldsymbol{A}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{A}\|_{F}^{2},

(30)

\displaystyle H_{L,B}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime};\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})

\displaystyle:=\left\langle\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{B},\nabla_{B}f_{n}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})\right\rangle+\frac{L}{2}\|\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{B}\|_{F}^{2}.

(31)

Assume the following inequality holds:

\displaystyle F_{n}\big(p_{L}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}),p_{L}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})\big)\geq

\displaystyle f_{n}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})+H_{L,B}(p_{L}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B});\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})

(32)

\displaystyle+H_{L,A}(q_{L}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B});\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})+\lambda_{n}\|p_{L}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})\|_{*}.

Then for any
\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times p_{2}}
and
\boldsymbol{A}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}\times k_{2}}
, we have

\displaystyle F_{n}\big(\boldsymbol{A}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}\big)-F_{n}\big(q_{L}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}),

\displaystyle p_{L}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})\big)\geq\frac{L}{2}\big(\|p_{L}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})-\boldsymbol{B}\|_{F}^{2}+\|q_{L}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})-\boldsymbol{A}\|_{F}^{2}\big)

(33)

\displaystyle+L\big(\left\langle\boldsymbol{B}-\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime},p_{L}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})-\boldsymbol{B}\right\rangle+\left\langle\boldsymbol{A}-\boldsymbol{A}^{\prime},q_{L}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})-\boldsymbol{A}\right\rangle\big).

Further, let
\{(\boldsymbol{A}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{B}^{(k)})\}_{k\geq 1}
be the sequences generated by Algorithm
1
. Then for any
k\geq 1
, we have

F_{n}(\boldsymbol{A}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{B}^{(k)})-F_{n}(\boldsymbol{A}^{*},\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\leq\frac{2\gamma L\big(\|\boldsymbol{A}^{(0)}-\boldsymbol{A}^{*}\|_{F}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{B}^{(0)}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{F}^{2}\big)}{(k+1)^{2}}.

(34)

Proof.

The
p_{L}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})
and
q_{L}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})
defined in Lemma
18
is equivalent to that defined in Section
2.2
, which can be shown using Lemma
17
and
Bertsekas et al. (1999)
, see also equation (5)-(7) in
Ji & Ye (2009)
.

The global convergence rate of Algorithm
1
is established by Lemma
18
, whose proves are direct extensions of Theorem 4.1 in
Ji & Ye (2009)
and are therefore omitted.
∎

Appendix C
Technical Assumptions

The following assumptions are needed for technical purposes.
Assumption
19
and
20
imposes conditions on factors and idiosyncratic components, still allowing weak correlations.
Assumption
21
is already satisfied by the data-driven method we have studied by its construction and by Proposition
7
. Nevertheless, since some other reasonable projection matrices can also be used, we impose Assumption
21
for genreality.
Assumption
22
is needed for asymptotic normality.

Assumption 19
.

For MFM (
1
), there exists universal constants
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
such that

(a)

The elements of
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}\times k_{2}}
